ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e357	For four years, the Transport and Communications Committee has travelled around Norway. We have visited all 19 counties of the country, met hundreds of rapporteurs and met even more representatives of business and business. We have eaten on board ferries in the West – ferries that in a few years are to be replaced by bridges and tunnels that bind the region together. We've been at the bottom of Oslofjord and we've been demonstrating the latest of laser technology to control the traffic flow in the Bjørvika tunnel. We have been to the driving cab on the Fly train and got a taste of what it is like to drive trains at 250 km/h in the Norwegian countryside. In the north, we have seen how climate change can give us a new world map and new transport routes through the northeast. All over the place, we've met a lot of commitment. We have been presented for recordings and demands, frustrations and expectations. We have become familiar with the challenges of transport and the needs both in individual regions and at national level. It is this knowledge that the Committee has used in its work on the government's proposal for a new national transport plan, which has resulted in a 250-page attitude to the Stortinget. I would like to thank the Committee for its very good cooperation. The deadlines have been short, and the work has been intensive, but we have set targets. Although this debate is most likely to focus on what we disagree on, we still agree on the important thing. We all share the same impatience after building the infrastructure in our country – faster, safer, simpler and more efficient. The Government’s proposal for a national transport plan is a historic boost. 508 B.C.E. kr – an increase of 50 pst. – must go to faster and safer infrastructure throughout the country. Once the Committee has traveled around and met representatives from the business community, there are two issues that have been addressed. The first is transportation. That's why I feel that the red-green parties have passed the exam with shine when the NHO writes in its consultation response that the government proposes a higher investment framework than the NHO did, and that the organisation feels this is an important breakthrough for better business relations. The goal of the red-green parties is clear: we are going to bind Norway together. We're going to spend 311 billion. kr for road purposes over the next 10 years. 1280 km of new national road will be opened for traffic, and 780 km will be built as a non-convenient road. We're going to rust up E6 all over the country, from Svinesund in the south to the Churches in the north. This means, among other things: that the most razed section of the entire E6, Nordnesfjellet in Kåfjord municipality in Troms, is finally secured. The Norwegian road network shall be developed in such a way as to provide good standard for longer continuous sections. That is why we also prioritise E18, E10, E16 and E134. We're going to build a ferry-free E39 in 20 years. Since 2005, travel time on this important coastal route has been reduced by 50 minutes. The projects within the plan will lead to a further 1.5 hours reduction in travel time. When the ferry-free E39 is a reality, the journey time will be reduced by more than seven hours. The county councils, which are responsible for large parts of the road network, are also helped to resume the maintenance set-up – with almost 20 billion. kr. The cost of transport for business is reduced to 78 billion. kr. That is why the transport plan is good business policy. Travel time between parts of the country is being cut sharply; it is also a good welfare policy. We want to build a new generation of railway for new generations of travelers. We're going to spend 168 billion. kr on the railway during the ten-year period. Full intercity expansion – an old vision – is finally realized. In 2024 there shall be continuous double tracks to Tønsberg, Fredrikstad and Hamar. The travel time from Oslo to these cities is less than 1 hour for thousands of passengers. Halden, Lillehammer and Skien shall have their double tracks ready by 2030. There is a green light for the Bergensbanen, modernisation and electrification of the Tronderbanen and expansion of the capacity of Ofotbanen. The red-green majority has also clarified in the comments that there is no need for a new KVU for the Ringerix track. The railway works are now required to complete the knowledge base so that we can start construction of the Ringeriksbahn at the earliest possible time in the second period. The big cities will experience a strong population growth in the years to come. That's why we're now giving cities dragons, so that all the growth in passenger traffic can be taken by public transport, corridors and bicycles, in line with the climate agreement. For the first time, a government says that the state must also take a partial responsibility for the investment and operation of public transport in the big cities, and deposit a total of 26 billion. kr to the urban environment agreements. The first ship tunnel in the world becomes a reality with this NTP. City Ship Tunnel will help improve both safety and accessibility for all who travel on the City Sea, and has been an important requirement for people along the coast. In order to move more goods across the sea, the government is giving 3 billion dollars. to develop port infrastructure and short sea shipping. We dance on the decks of ships in joy, was the feedback from the maritime environments after the Prime Minister had presented the news. We're using almost 15 billion today. kr more in the year on the road and track than when we took over in 2005. However, increased frameworks impose increased requirements, so red-green batches are concerned that resources should be used as efficiently as possible and that we should have a modern sector. Today too much is being built in jerks and beaks. The planning time shall be halved, inter alia: through increased use of State plan, better coordination of State authorities' input and coordinated processing of KVU/KS1. We will ensure more predictable funding and implementation, where the main projects have their own organisations, own decision-making with total commitments and annual appropriations and completion dates. This has also been very well received by the business community. The NHO believes, inter alia, that: new schemes show that the government has once again responded to business needs. But it is not natural that this transport plan should be as solid as it is. When the red-green parties took over the government offices in 2005 after the Bondevik II government, we also assumed responsibility for a transport policy that had been underfunded and not given priority. The first thing we did was we did all the projects and overfulfill the plans that had been adopted. There were no governments that had done before us. The second was to raise the level of ambition. National transport plan 2010–2019 is approximately 100 billion. kr greater than the transport plan of the Bondevik II Government. Now we're adding another 150 billion. kr on top. The third thing we've also done in these eight years is finally to use resources on maintenance. This has led to results: more than nine out of ten trains are on the way, and we have asphalted more than one third of the highways since 2010. The red-green government has implemented a shift in its policy on transport. We've increased the traffic budget by 64 pst. The Bondevik II government increased it by 10 pst. We have doubled the allocation to the railway and tripled the investment. Nearly 1000 km of motorway is open or under construction. We've built the fraction of what's of the non-conference road in Norway. And perhaps the most joyous result: the number of fatalities and injuries has been reduced by 30 pst, to the lowest since 1950, despite the fact that traffic has doubled. Last year 148 people were killed, and for the first time since statistics were started, one state was left at the end of the year without killing anyone in traffic, Finnmark. We're going to do it again. Just as we have fulfilled the two transport plans for which we have been responsible, so, year by year, project for project, we will also implement this plan. We're going to take Norway further. On the other hand, what the opposition agrees on in the policy on transport is more unclear. The right is for tolls, for the rule of action, for climate settlement and for OPS. The progress party is against tolls, against the rule of action, against climate change settlement and against OPS as financing form. The progress party will spend more than 400 billion dollars. kr more than the right and the other opposition lots. ‘It’s going to trick people into thinking that we’re going to end up near the sums proposed by the Progress Party.’ This was said by Committee Chairman Knut Arild Hareide to the Newspaper last week. And he went on to say, ‘Believing in that kind of sum is like believing in Christmas.’ And let me add: it's a party that really has experience with miracles. Right-wing transport policy spokesman Trond Helleland said to NTB that the disagreement between the Progress Party and the other opposition parties was a gordish knot and that it was difficult to see how the opposition parties were going to be able to find a common multiple. To be honest, it must be possible to expect a clarification of what kind of transport policy we will have with a blue-blue government. The extent of the opposition's transport policy, and its funding, are completely blue. One example clearly shows the gravity of the situation: the government's proposal contains a total of 174 road projects. 92 projects shall be financed without tolls and 82 toll projects — at the request of local politicians. Of the 82 projects to be co-financed with tolls, 25 are under construction. Then we are left with 56 new major road projects, where the Right and Advance Party are fully involved in a collision course on funding – important road projects across the country, such as the E6 Manglerud project, the E39 Rogfast and the Helgelands package on E6. The right does not include government appropriations in its option that can replace the toll share in these projects. The Progress Party has in no way suggested that they are ready to compromise on tolls. Would the Right and the Advance Party agree on the financing of these road projects, if they were to be in government? Will these 56 major road projects be carried out with a government made up of the Right Party and the Advance Party? NTP work has not provided any clarifications. The lot also disagrees on the use of OPS, public-private cooperation. In the VG we could read earlier in our reading that the representative Hoksrud slaughtered the Right OPS policy, because there is an expensive way to build a road. Today's Business is one of many newspapers that have concluded that the Right is using OPS as a magic agent and that the consequence is to push the bill in front of and over to the next generation. But when the representative of Greece was confronted with this by the class struggle, he answered the following: ‘It is appropriate to produce OPS projects as if the future taxpayer were to be billed or that they would bind future governments in such a way that other projects would not be affected. It is primarily the car list that will get the bill, in the form of tolls”. I started by saying that there are two factors business has been concerned about when we traveled around with the transport committee. The first is transportation – which has been delivered by the government. The other is safe economic governance. Therefore, the Workers' Party is not willing to break the rules of action and push costs in the sector into the wind, which would be the result of the Progress Party's policy. Therefore, the Workers' Party is not willing to use the Right Wizard as OPS. I fully agree with what was written in the evening post manager last week: ‘The level of transport must be prioritised within the framework of the rules of action for the use of oil money.’ As a form of funding, OPS is at best a tempting but risky experiment. This can easily lead to high capital costs being passed on to future generations, and governments will be tied up, because we are unable to prioritise among the many good purposes of policy. In the short term, of course, it's provocative – the ghost that can come later, it's not. The road here has been long. A lot of people are involved, and there's a lot of commitment. When I look up at the gallery, it's nice to see that people have decorated themselves on occasion of this day, because it's a party day. Today, projects that are important, many of which are involved, many of them behind, are being adopted throughout the country, which will make great differences for both business and population. With the NTP proposal, the government indicates that we are now moving on to Norway.

The red-green government seems to have prioritised bureaucracy. Compared to our neighbouring countries, Norway now has twice as many traffic bureaucrats compared to the population. For example, since 2009, the State Road Administration has employed more than 1000 people. We heard last week that the Railway Agency has received 1000 new ones in eight years, all in administrative positions. The road service spent 2.4 billion. kr on the purchase of consultants only in 2012. It is important, of course, that there are plenty of staff with the right skills so that the Road Administration and the Railway Agency can keep good progress and the necessary control of investment and operation. At the same time, we see that the number of road bureaucrats in Norway is highest in the Nordic region, even if we look at the number of employees in clean numbers, or by the number of kilometres of road. In fact, in Sweden, road bureaucrats manage more than twice as many kilometres of roads per employee than their Norwegian counterparts. This shows that there are considerable opportunities for efficiency in road and rail management. While in Norway there are about 14,000 traffic bureaucrats, the same figures in Denmark and Finland are slightly over 4,000. Public–private cooperation provides more money, shorter construction time and high quality infrastructure. Therefore, the Right wishes to launch several major OPS projects both on the road and on the railway. Norway has had three public-private cooperation projects, OPS, on the road sector. The experience from these has been very good. Roads have, according to an evaluation by TØI and Dovre International, been completed faster than with traditional implementation. The construction method – and I stress the construction method – public-private cooperation can offer several advantages: half-time, continuous development of long distances, guaranteed development costs, no budget gaps for the state, can be more reasonable than traditional development, construction quality adapted to the overall life of the road or line because the developer himself is responsible for the maintenance for 25 years, if one goes into such a contract, guarantee of proper maintenance as long as the OPS contract runs, and boost innovation in the choice of solutions. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e1153	Today, Stortinget adopts a document that will change Norway. National transport plan 2014-2023 will contribute to meeting important transport needs across the country. A new transport plan will enable a safer, faster and simpler transport system. It will contribute to a modern purpose. Central parts of the railway will have a modern standard. InterCity will be expanded, and Norwegian aviation will be further developed to maintain its good position. Politics is not about promising the most, nor about using the greatest words, but about implementing what you promise. In 2005, the red-green opposition accumulated and proposed 22 billion. more for the journey than the right party of the Christian people, and the left. This was characterised by the Bondevik government as a surrender. In the 2009 budget, the government could show that the promise was overfilled by nearly 6 billion. kr. That same year, the government presented a new big journey promise, and today it is promised a fulfilled promise, even overfilled by 1.5 billion. kr. With today’s National Transport Plan, ambitions are further lifted. The financial framework for the transport plan will be increased by 50 p.m. in relation to the 2013 appropriations, and we are taking structural action to ensure that we get more for each ticket. The transport system will be simpler because we want to simplify planning processes and ensure rational implementation. This will be faster because large expansions and investments in operation and maintenance will bring significant travel time gains. And it'll be safer because we'll continue our efforts on road safety. Here we still want to be far ahead. Today’s approach shows that there is a broad consensus on the long-term objectives of Norwegian transport policy: accessibility, road safety, environment and universal design. Neither is there any significant disagreement with regard to the overall financial framework. The greatest disagreement in the financing of transport does not go between government and opposition, but it goes between opposition parties. The government is concerned that we will get more money back in the transport sector. I am pleased that the Stortinget today joins in a new and predictable way of financing large transport facilities. A secure financing from the day one will mean rational progress, faster completion and saving money. This transport plan defines to a far greater extent the transport operations to be undertaken by different modes of transport. We depend on both aviation, maritime transport, road and rail in this country. And the modes of transport must work together. Road will be the cornerstone of transport in large parts of the country, just as public transport will be the best way of managing the growth of urban areas. The railway must first be built out where the railway has its maximum potential. Once the plan has been implemented, the railway will make it much easier to commute over greater distances and thus help to reduce urban pressure problems. There is no difference between town and town. The deployment of the railway effort is important for decommissioning cities, but also important for further growth in rural areas. In the future, aviation will be central to the longest journeys. What Norway sees today, with housing and business throughout the country, depends on a fine-skinned airport structure. A new transport plan ensures that. It is very ambitious when the Stortinget today decides that the growth of passenger travel in metropolitan areas should be taken by public transport, by bicycle and by bicycle. This objective, as well as the new urban environment agreements to be negotiated subsequently between the major cities and the state, will change urban areas. The transport plan suggests that road investment should be increased by 77 pst. from the level in 2013. 1280 km of highway shall be built during the ten-year period. Of this, 780 km are to be built as a non-conference road. That means that the number of kilometres of road without meeting will double in the period. The tolls will be around the day's level for the next ten years. On the other hand, government appropriations for road investment will increase. So the total share of road investment in tolls will fall sharply over the next 10 years. Railway investments shall be increased by 51 pst. from the level in 2013. We often have a tendency in the transport debate to point to the expectations ahead, and then the parties double the expectations. But I also think it's important to take into account that there has been a formidable investment on the railways in the last eight years. Compared to 2005, the planned increase in railway investment is a total of 380 pst. The journey time on the IC routes will be greatly reduced during the period. From Oslo to Skien, travel time will be reduced by 37 minutes, from Oslo to Lillehammer by 29 minutes and from Oslo to Halden by 18 minutes. As important as building new, it's taking care of what we have. It is set to increase the annual level of funds for road operation and maintenance by nearly 18 pst. compared to the level in 2013. This will reduce the maturity for the first time during the planning period. For the railway it is added up to a level of operation and maintenance which is close to 46 pst. higher than in 2013. This means that the number of hours that trains will be delayed will be greatly reduced, and the number of trains cancelled will also be greatly reduced. Traffic safety is an important part of the national transport plan and the broad and systematic work undertaken in recent years to reduce the risk of accidents must continue. Since 2005, the accident risk has been reduced by 30 pst. The ambitious objective of the planning period is to halve the number of fatalities and road traffic damage. Investments in the transport plan will also reduce transport costs for business by 78 billion. kr during the 10 year period. For districts, transport costs will be reduced by 47 billion. kr. The NHO and the LO have defined the government’s transport plan as a historic boost. The NHO believes that the investment framework set up by the government is far from the scope of action in the economy. In other words, the government bets what it is possible to invest in the next ten years, without destroying Norwegian competitiveness for business. The government has high ambitions in the field of transport, and we have – so far – fulfilled them. Nearly 1,000 km of new highway is already built, or under construction. Large parts of InterCity are already under construction. Punctuality on the railway has improved significantly. Nine out of ten trains are now in the route. Climate emissions from the transport sector do not increase, despite strong traffic growth. The transport plan adopted today will change Norway. It will contribute to a faster, simpler and safer transport system and will provide the basis for a forward-looking transport policy, where needs across the country are met. All transport needs we have in Norway are not covered by this 10-year plan, but it shows – literally – the way to a simpler, safer and faster transport system.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e1409	Connecting our country together has been an important task at all times. With politics and technology, we have, through generations, despite natural forces and overcome our demanding landscape. A major boost has been made in the field of transport, but there are still significant challenges ahead of us. We must therefore take care of the values already laid down in the transport infrastructure. At the same time, it is important to invest in new infrastructure that builds on value creation and more jobs and that binds the country even more closely. National Transport Plans 2014-2023 focus on measures both in cities and in districts so that the whole country can participate in positive societal developments. Norway is a maritime and coastal nation. Our marine areas are about seven times larger than mainland Norway, and the coastline of bays and fjords is longer than the circumference of the earth around the equator. And most of us have a relationship with the coast and the sea: the sea gives us food and energy and is an important transport year. Many live and work along the coast, and even more spend their leisure time there. Our strength and position as a maritime and coastal nation is not only due to natural conditions, but can also be attributed to our will and efforts to expand infrastructure along the coast. Such an infrastructure is important for the petroleum activity, for industrial workplaces, for fisheries, for the maritime food industry and not least for the tourism industry. Let me also say that maritime transport is an important and well-functioning part of the Norwegian transport system. More than 40 pst. of the total domestic cargo work carried out by ships and, in international trade, maritime transport is totally dominant. However, it is that more goods can and should be carried by sea, because maritime transport is associated with low infrastructure costs, maritime transport limits land use, and maritime transport can address queues, congestion and maintenance and investment needs on the roads. Furthermore, maritime transport is a safe form of transport which also contributes to safer road transport by removing several heavy vehicles from the road. Good infrastructure is – combined with maritime services, such as: loss and monitoring of ship traffic — key maritime safety measures that contribute to the prevention of marine collisions, ground collisions and adverse marine incidents. In addition, ships are effective – with low energy consumption and low greenhouse gas emissions. That is why there are good reasons why the government will see that as much growth as possible in the long freight transport is on track and sea, and that we will pursue a policy to facilitate the transport of goods from road to sea. I am therefore very pleased that in the National Transport Plan 2014-2023 there is an offensive investment of 19.4 billion. kr directed towards the sea and maritime transport. It's equivalent to an increase of 55 pst. in relation to the 2013 budget, the increase is 180 pst. if one compares with the level in 2005. That is, the level is almost three times as high as it was when the red-green government took over in 2005. For the maritime transport sector, the Government has these main priorities in the National Transport Plan 2014-2023: actions in a dedicated short sea shipping strategy on fairway improvements — including the world’s first maritime tunnel on maritime traffic monitoring operations, maintenance and renewal of port infrastructure navigation aids in Longyearbyen and the development of BarentsWatch on transport planning and coastal management research and investigation on freight transport and combined transport solutions. Every year it passes half a million trailers across the Svinesund, and we know that freight will increase significantly in the coming years. The government has therefore set out in its national transport plan of 3 billion. kr for measures to increase the transport of goods by sea. For every ship being filled, we save the roads – and protect those who travel there – for about 200 trailers. Within the framework of a short sea shipping strategy, we will design measures to stimulate the increased use of short sea shipping, to design a grant scheme for State aid for investments in designated ports, and to design a port cooperation and freight concentration subsidy scheme. And not least, we want to strengthen research and investigation on the transport of goods by sea and combined transport solutions, because, as you know, most goods have longer transport needs than from one port to the next – we need to find the good combined transport solutions. The first three points that I mentioned here represent something completely new. This is a new area of activity which will help to clarify and make the Coastal Network visible as a port and maritime transport body. These days work on concreteising the measures as far as possible within the State aid guidelines. The plan is to present the strategy in August. The final design and implementation of the measures will depend on the ESA treatment. In addition, we shall, in cooperation with carriers and owners of goods, identify freight flows, logistical trends and the interaction between key actors in the freight market. The aim is to provide the best possible basis for assessing the potential for efficient transport of goods by land by sea. In summary, we believe that we have delivered a transport plan for the coast with which there is reason to be very satisfied. It's also the feedback we get from the different actors. At the same time, it is quite clear that we cannot stand here in this room and adopt more goods at sea. We are totally dependent on economic operators helping to achieve this goal and at the same time finding solutions that are attractive to transport buyers. The transport plan discussed here today will mean a great deal for the maritime sector, and it builds on environmental policy and industrial development throughout the country. With the policy that the government has now drawn up in the national transport plan, it is designed to enable carriers and owners of goods to assess maritime transport with new eyes.

This will provide investment in designated ports, better cooperation and division of tasks between ports, strengthening research and distribution on the carriage of goods by sea, and facilitate good links between the various modes of transport. Safety and emerging fairways are crucial for maritime transport. Upgrading and investing in maritime infrastructure and securing fairways will, together with a forward-looking, cost-effective and user-friendly unloading service, be an important contribution to a safer maritime transport service. Not all investments in infrastructure are visible, but unless the fairways are secured and dangerous grounds are blown away, it is well marked when the ship goes down. Developments are in the direction of an increasing number of types of ships, and several of the fairways along our coast are also being used by deep-sea special vessels and for the towing of oil installations. A good example here is the measure to extend the seal to Florø, which makes it possible to seal larger rigs to the base of Florø. In NTP, nearly 300 MIU should be used. kr annually for fishing ports and for local fishing port facilities. This is a very important factor in ensuring good infrastructure for the fishing industry and for maritime transport in the districts. This plan also provides for me to now build the world's first tunnel for ships, and it's set at 1 billion. kr to City Ship Tunnel. This is a major effort that will help to strengthen and secure maritime transport along our coasts in general, and for all those who travel on the City Sea in particular. The majority of the Committee stated that further quality assurance of the project, KS2, and planning were carried out with a view to the start of the project in the period from 2018. I might have boasted even more of the investment in maritime transport, but it may also be worth seeing what other central maritime actors report in their hearings. Norway’s Shipowners’ Association has high expectations of both the maritime strategy and the forecasting of the short sea shipping strategy, saying that expectations are reinforced by the government’s long-term commitment to a more offensive policy towards short sea shipping. NHO Maritime states that they are seeing a happy development, with increasing attention to measures that can strengthen maritime transport, and that NTP provides a positive signal of action to get more goods across from land to sea. One of the largest national grips in NTP is the ambition to bind the coast together through an upgraded and free E39 from Stavanger to Trondheim in 20 years. Several important and large projects for upgrading between the ferry sections of the E39 are part of the government's proposal, and the holiday settlement project Rogfast is now starting in the first part of the period. It is implemented KVU and KS1 for Hordfast, Nordfjorden, Storfjorden, Romsdalsfjorden and Halsafjorden. These areas have a very active and comprehensive business life that needs good transport years to maintain and further develop activity and that needs good labour supply. The economy is also dependent on different international economic cycles, and a larger commuting area will clearly strengthen these regions. Between Bergen and Stavanger, we find the largest bus and work area outside the Oslo area, and the realization of Hordfast will mean a coherent ferry-free path on this whole nutrient-rich and popular route. Similarly, Møreaksen will provide large savings in an active, nutrient-intensive area with a high potential for regional enlargement. Common to Hordfast and Møreaksen are high traffic numbers and the closure of several ferry connections. These will be important elements in the total funding of the projects. It is very good that the means of planning are now used when the concept options for the different fjord crossings are agreed. It is also very good that concrete projects at E39 are candidates for the new scheme for priority projects, which will provide more careful funding and more rational implementation. In addition, more effective progress could be made if state regulation were to be applied. The opposition calls for the Storting to adopt the concept of several of the fjord crossings today. Of course, the government doesn't have to let these decisions go for too long, but I would like to point out that it is important to have proper quality assurance on such a large project. I would also like to remind you that the plan applies from 2014, so we have a little time. Planning is not yet the basis for determining the order of the other fjord crossings following Rogfast. However, I would like to point out that Hordfast, Nordfjorden and Møreaksen have to be planned for start-up clearance at the next roll-out of the plan, and that the plant start for these can then take place in the period from 2018. In committee hearings, we have received central input from several nation-wide organisations in the direction that the idea of ferry-free coastal lineage must be dropped, and that we can manage these ferries. But for us who live by the coast, it would be to be against the ferry-free coastal line as if we were to lie on the ceiling and be against the stairs. The NTP submitted for 2014-2023 is a good plan, and the ferries-free coastal lineway is one of the most important elements of the plan.

There are even fewer killed and severely injured Norwegian roads now than was the stage objective of the current national transport plan. Although the number of 145 killed in 2012 is 145 too many, it's the lowest since 1950. That's 30 pst. lower than in 2005. In addition, traffic has increased by 12 pst. Yesterday came the European Transport Safety Council, ETSC, with its report on road safety in Europe in 2012, «Back on track to reach the EU 2020 Road Safety Target?» According to their figures, Norway is part of the Council road safety league and is at the end of 2012 no. 3 in Europe in traffic safety when we look at the number of deaths compared to the population — only shut down by Malta and the United Kingdom. We have both Sweden and Denmark behind us. When the number of deaths at the end of May was six lower than at the same time in 2012, we see that the positive trend continues. Now, when we're as far down in the number of fatalities and injuries in traffic as we have, it's ambitious to set a stage target of halving the number of fatalities and hard injuries in the planning period compared to the previous planning period. But the stage goal is set, and fortunately, I feel that there is a cross-political uprising on this goal. On the other hand, what that is not a cross-political agreement is how we are going to achieve the goal. I'll come back to that. We need to move on in the right direction in terms of road safety and further reduce the number of fatalities and injuries. That requires a lot of us who are politicians. This will require courageous decisions, which will not necessarily be considered positive. I would like to point out that the politicians who, at the end of the 1960s, adopted the directive on safety belts, which was introduced in 1971, were actually courageous politicians. They adopted a decision that was very controversial, of which there was considerable disagreement and which was not particularly popular — but which has proved to be one of the most important individual decisions that have a positive impact on road safety. There is no one today who believes that it was wrong. I believe that we will have several such belt cases that we will take a decision on. There may be alcolocks, speed barriers or other forms of new technology that will give us safer traffic. The question is whether the representatives in this Chamber will be as courageous as our former colleagues were at the end of the 1960s. The zero vision is based on a traffic system where the road system, the vehicle and the road user have to play on layers and where everyone has to do their part in the job for it to work. As politicians, we can be involved and influence cooperation – by helping to improve road users, better and safer vehicles and better and safer roads. We can also adopt measures that compensate for the failure of parts of the system to function optimally, or for not having to take the measures that we believe are necessary. I believe that in any case we cannot manage to build roads that take into account that road users are allowed to behave as they please. We need to be able to assume — or at least to implement measures to help — that road users generally hold the speed limits, use the seat belt and drive unattended. We here in the house are responsible for the roads, to grant money and to ensure that the money that we grant is spent in a way that provides the most possible road safety. However, we are also responsible for legislation and measures that give us safer and better road users – better driver training, traffic training and awareness-raising work in schools and kindergartens through framework plans and curricula and, what is introduced from the fall of, traffic as an electoral profession in high school. Many accidents are related to lack of driving ability, excessive speed or driving in a drug-induced condition. This means that we need to put in place measures to improve road users. For us, this is about action – from going to kindergarten, and until one is fully trained driver. That is why we need to focus all the time on: Is the traffic training in kindergartens and in the schoolwork good enough? In primary schools, we have learning objectives in traffic curricula, but do we achieve the learning goals? Is driving training good enough? What we can determine is that we have the world's best education for traffic instructors. We have chosen to have traffic teachers who are trained at the university level, and place great emphasis on pedagogics and road safety gains. No other Nordic country has gone that far. But what about the driver training — do we have a driver training that takes more account of the fact that it should be as cheap as possible instead of being as optimal as possible? Do we have a driver training that is equal to all personality types, regardless of how we are as human beings? When I talk to the traffic teacher training professionals at the Nord-Trøndelag High School, I get the following feedback on what can be done to improve driver training: we have to reintroduce the mandatory theoretical knowledge course, we have to make sure that it becomes more intensive training, and we have to make sure that we get a driver training adapted to the human being. These are signals that are important to take with them when improving driver training. We are also responsible for the speed limit policy. It shall comply with the zero vision concept. This is why it is important to signal a positive political attitude to the increasing use of so-called zero-vision speed limits, i.e. 30 km/h where there are many soft road users, and 70 km/h where there is a lot of traffic and no middle parts. As politicians, we have to stand up and defend this, because it's the body's tolerability that determines these speed limits, not how fast each one of us wants to drive, or how good the standard on the road seems to be. If road safety were most important to us, this would have been a good discussion. But I know it's going against the goal of prosperity, and that it probably won't be popular with the voters – but we know that it will work. If we had lowered the speed limits from 50 to 30 in densely built strips and from 80 to 70 where there are no pitfalls, and everyone had followed the speed limits, we would have killed over 100 fewer people each year – over 100. By comparison, estimates show that when we complete the construction of semi-trailers on all roads with traffic over 8 000 cars per day, we reduce the number of accidents with fatalities and heavy injuries by 35-40. When a parking lot is built on all roads with traffic over 6000 cars per day, we get a further reduction, with 15 fatalities or heavy injuries per year. The working party will build more mints. However, we also know that building a parking lot on all roads with over 6,000 cars per day and with a speed limit of more than 70 km/h roughly estimated will cost 300 billion kroner. kr. The progress party will use 1 billion. kr more in the year to build a mint. In other words, it will take 300 years before there is a pitfall on all Norwegian roads with ÅDT over 6000. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e3282	This is a joyous day for Vestfold. The extension of the line Bommestad-Sky is the final phase of the E18 development in Vestfold. When the road is finished in 2017, we have a continuous four-field road from Oslo to Telemark. The new road provides for increased road safety, improved traffic environment and traffic flows, and, in some cases, the E18 will significantly reduce driving time and reduce transport costs for business. Together with the development of the railways, which is also in full swing, a new and timely infrastructure will improve the conditions for housing and industrial development throughout the west of the Oslofjorden. It's needed in a state where we'll probably be 100,000 more people in the years to 2030. But the road has its price – 4.2 billion. kr, and the drivers shall pay most of the costs. The state's share is now 28.5 p.m., and when the road is finished with its seven toll stations, from Telemark to Gulli at Tønsberg, it will cost 88 kroner and 176 kroner respectively to pass the toll stations with a light and heavy vehicle. And although we currently pay 90 kroner to drive the route Nuttøøy–Oslo, knowing that there are toll projects in the west and north at higher rates, I have a great understanding that those who have to use E18 in the south of Westfold daily seem to be expensive. I therefore value the attempts made by the county to reduce the bomb charges. In particular, this applies to the conclusion of lending agreements with favourable interest rates, which will help to reduce future financing costs. This is quite important, because tariffs depend to a large extent on measures to improve the traffic environment and road safety on the cross-roads. That's how the bulk of the population actually plays the ball back to the county. Calculations I have been given part in show that the tolls can be reduced by up to NOK 20 if the traffic leak is removed completely. Based on Klinestad and Fokserød, where an estimated 10,000 cars per day will use the side roads once the toll station is in place, the estimates show that the rates could be reduced by 2 kr per 1000 cars which instead choose to drive E18. However, measures on the cross-road network are mainly the responsibility of Vestfold county councils, and I believe that remedial measures on the cross-roads network and their effects should have been the basis for the Storting's treatment of this proposition. In the extension of the current decision in the Stortinget, the county should, in my opinion, clarify the measures needed to reduce the traffic leakage from new E18 to the secondary roads in order to reduce the handicaps to the inhabitants along the side roads and to reduce traffic leakage. This also has a clear safety aspect. The construction of a new and safe road with good capacity is of little help if tolls lead to the introduction of a large part of the traffic on a road network which is not at all dimensioned for this purpose. It is usually not my job to guide the state politicians in Westfold in this or other matters, and I understand, of course, that it may be difficult to choose between unpopular measures such as the closure of side roads, the reduction of lateral protrusion by means of physical measures, or the setting up of bombs on the main side roads following patterns from E18 in Sande. However, it is equally necessary to make such assessments and take such decisions.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e3311	It is very positive that the Stortinget today makes decisions on the development and financing of E18 on the route Bommestad-Sky. This 7 km long stretch in Larvik is the last development stage of the E18 through Vestfold, and it is planned to start construction until the autumn and open for traffic in 2017. The project will deliver significant benefits in terms of improved road safety, smoother traffic flows and improvement of the environment along the road. There shall be no concealment that there has been disagreement on the choice of solutions on the route Bombestad-Sky. Following the confirmation by the Ministry of the Environment of the municipal sub-plan in 2010, a longer tunnel than initially assumed between Bommestad and the Farriseidet has been built at local request, and a more expensive solution has also been chosen with the sloped bridge at the Farriseidet. This, together with some other requirements and solutions, has resulted in large increases in costs and, in this context, increased toll tariffs have been notified. It has always been provided that the state framework should be maintained if a more expensive solution to the route was chosen than expected. It's covered both in St.prp. No 60 for 2008-2009 and also for Prp. 56 S for 2010–2011. The State contribution to the overall expansion of Gulli-Langåker and Bombestad-Sky-Langåker is approximately 2.3 billion. kr, and it is provided as provided for in the original financing plan of St.prp. No 60. There has been some local resistance to the expected fare level on the route, but local authorities have previously accepted a fare of NOK 106 at the 2011 level. However, on the basis of strong local wishes, the Ministry of Transport has also accepted a lower calculation technical interest rate assumption in this case than is usual in toll propositions. It has resulted in a significant reduction in the tariff level, which, in the context of the proposition, has now been calculated at NOK 88 in 2013 level for a normal car. There is a comprehensive parallel network along the E18 in Westfold, and high toll tariffs may result in traffic being rejected or another route being taken. It can bring benefits to society, and the Ministry will therefore take the initiative to review toll tariffs once Bombestad-Sky is opened. The final costs of the project and the traffic base will then be well known. Thus, there could also be significantly greater security around conditions affecting the economy of the tolling company. In addition, the State Road Service will continuously monitor this situation and also consider other measures to reduce traffic rejection. When Bombestad-Sky is finished in four years, there will be continuous four-field road from Oslo to Telemark. Completion of the E18 through Westfold will reduce driving time, improve predictability and reduce transport costs and can also contribute to regional enlargement. It is also the case that the road safety benefits will be significant. For large parts of E18 through Westfold, a reduction in the number of fatalities and damage to about 70 pst. is now expected, with the greatest reduction for the major accidents. This is a main source of transport in Westfold, but also between the East and Grenland and the South. With the completion of the development, it will be arranged so that a significant part of the traffic both in the district of Westfold and also through Westfold can go on the high-standard four-field road. It's very positive.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4072	It is a pleasure that Stortinget now agrees that Trondheim can go further with step 2 of the environmental package. Step 2 is a continuation and extension of step 1, which was dealt with at Stortinget in 2009. Steps 1 and 2 form the whole of the environmental package and have been addressed in the context of the proposal. Step 1 of the environmental package was presented in St.prp. No 85 for 2008-2009 and was dealt with in the Stortinget in June 2009. Step 1 funds have mainly been used for two major road projects and a number of measures involving pedestrian and cyclist roads and public transport. Step 2 essentially implies a continuation and extension of step 1. The Trondheim Environmental Package includes, inter alia: the development of a relieving main road system around Trondheim and E6 south of the city. In addition, measures to facilitate public transport, walking and cycling are included. Priority is also given to road safety and the environment. The environmental package includes, inter alia: the aim of reducing car traffic and increasing journeys by environmentally friendly transport. It has been so far successful. Car traffic has been reduced by approx. 10 pst. since the toll collection started in 2010, and public transport has increased by about half. 40 pst. since 2008. The environmental package Trondheim is a collaboration with the financial contributions of the state, Sør-Trøndelag county municipality and Trondheim municipality, as well as contributions in the form of tolls. Step 2 provides a balanced balance between the necessary road development and the strengthening of environmentally friendly transport. The tolling system is extended by several tolling stations, and it is an action that will restrict car traffic but at the same time increases revenue to finance the development of a more environmentally friendly and efficient transport system. 16 new toll stations are planned in addition to today's 7. All toll stations are charged 24 hours a day and in both directions. The system of higher tariffs in the rush hour is going to be continued. It is important to stress that, after the new toll scheme has been in operation for one year, an evaluation shall be carried out to assess whether the toll collection and use of the tolling materials are designed to comply with the principle of efficiency. This applies, inter alia, to: in particular the relationship between Trondheim and surrounding municipalities. The extension of E6 Jakøya-Klett-Tonstad and Rv. 706 Release–Stavne is part of the Environmental Package Trondheim and has a total priority of 1.6 billion. kr in government funds in the national transport plan, which we have just dealt with. In addition, government funding goes over the programme areas, and government funding from the reward scheme and from the new scheme of overall urban environmental agreements is also relevant. The Trondheim Environmental Package means that work on removing public transport bottlenecks will continue, including: by establishing collective fields and upgrading current nodes and universally designed holding sites and improved traffic information. For those who come to Trondheim in a few years' time, the transformation of the Elgeseter Gate into a high-standard collective gate will appear to be a very wise solution. It is also added to renew the track between St. Olav's Gate and Ila. Like the majority of the committee, I believe that it is a strength for the Trondheim Environmental Package that there is a broad political consensus on step 2 and that almost the entire Stortinget, with the exception of the Progress Party, is behind the proposition. Finally, I would like to thank the Stortinget for a quick handling of the matter and also congratulate everyone in the Trondheim area who will benefit from the next step of the Trondheim Environmental Package.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4151	These proposals go to the heart of perhaps the most important lines of Norwegian policy. It is based on labour policy and on the pillars of the Norwegian model, which deal with the distribution of power and money in society – a pillar of community and welfare. The threat is quite obvious in these proposals presented by the Left, and if the rest of the opposition does not show the cards in the setting, we know that they are far on the way in many of these questions following the Left. It is at the heart of some of what has ensured safety in Norway, namely the trade union movement. It is intended to weaken its foundations by making more regulations in the labour market universal and not depending on whether the workplace has a collective agreement. The collective bargaining agreements are also strongly challenged by the release of job protection and clear rules on working time and the working environment, that they should apply to everyone regardless of whether they are organised or not, i.e. the possibility of having alternative schemes. In our view, this is a great threat to this bearer in Velferds-Norway. A civil government will make a big difference in this area. It can be done if one does not promote concrete proposals – it is in a way enough to hold hands, especially when one sees changes in the labour market with social dumping and heavy pressure on the rights of workers, which are speeding up the pace due to the financial crisis and increasing pressure from many unemployed people across Europe. A decent working life must be followed up and kept in claim every single day. The premise for some of these proposals is that working life has changed. Yes, it is, but not essentially as the Left describes it, namely that most people can decide their working hours seam and do parts of the job from the iPad on the cabin. No, most people have to be in the store, they have to be in the hospital, they have to teach at school, they have to drive the bus – they have to serve the functions they're going to be in the community. But the labour market is heavily changed, with large social dumping that threatens working life. That is why a so-called decoration of the rules proposed by the Left and, as I hear, large parts of both the Right and the Advance Party are also taking the floor, will change Norwegian working life – and then to the worse. The claim and the premise is that the rules are too rigid. No, the rules aren't rigid. There is a lot of flexibility in the rules as they are today, but a sort of step-by-step model has been created, where the possibilities of exceptions to the general rules are much greater if you are a trade union or have a large association behind you. It is precisely because we know that the individual worker is sometimes pressured beyond his ability to work. It is also because we know that there is no uniform relationship of strength between an employee and an employer, which is an enterprise. This level playing field on which the proposal is based is not present unless both the strength of the trade union movement and its ability to handle legislation jointly. I do not have time to review each proposal, but mainly I can say that a general access to temporary employment up to 12 months – as the Left wishes – will allow for much more temporary employees. It is the strong legal requirement for permanent employment that helps temporary staff to obtain permanent employment. This is the context in which temporary work can lead to permanent work. It is also the case that the requirement of agreement with employee organisations in terms of flexibility in working time rules is deliberately made because individual workers are not to be pressured into agreements that break with the main concern – that is, to have a working life that also takes people’s health into account, and that people should be able to stand out throughout their working life and reach the retirement age as a worker.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4385	This proposal is not about pension reform. Let me make that clear. I have raised the matter several times in Stortinget. This is the last time I try to explain to Stortinget what this is all about – at least from this pulpit in this period – but let me try again. The old pension system, the old population benefit we had before 1 January 2011, was built up with three pillars: basic pension, supplementary pension and special supplement for those who had not worked long enough to earn a pension that was higher than the minimum level. Then it became so that everyone who was married or cohabiting got a curtailment of their basic pension. With the new pension scheme, these rules have been amended. The new pension system is based on two pillars in terms of earnings. It is your pension stock on the one hand that is to be compensated for a guarantee pension stock on the other. The amount of the guarantee pension is determined by the amount of income you have had. For 80 pst. the amount of the pension in respect of income shall be reduced to the amount of the guarantee pension in order to determine the amount of the guarantee pension to be added to the social security. It has also been said in the new system that curtailment based on marital status is still going to happen, but it is going to happen in the guarantee pension stock. And what are the consequences of this? Well, the consequences are that those who have had high incomes avoid curtailment. If you have an income of over 450 000 kroner and are married or cohabited with someone who has also had income higher than that on average, is 80 pst. of your pension stock higher than the guarantee pension stock. Ergo will not have any abbreviation. It really means that the bus driver married to the cleaner gets a full curtailment in the new system, while the doctor married to the director does not get a curtailment, but has full earnings just because 80 pst. the amount of the retirement pension is higher than the amount of the pension guarantee. Then I find it a little strange – and there I disagree with the case rapporteur – to hear the argument that the background to the pension agreement was that we had to have sustainability. We had to save money. The Norwegian economy could not carry that pension scheme on. Then it becomes strange to me that the same majority believe that we do not have the strength to remove the abbreviation for those with low incomes, while we have the strength to remove the abbreviation for those with high incomes. And the argument, which has been used in the past, that it's cheaper to live together than one alone, falls into the new system. Can the majority then explain to me why it is cheaper to live together for the director and doctor on the west edge of Oslo than for the cleaner and bus driver on the east edge of Oslo? The former omits the abbreviation, the latter gets it. So that argument also falls completely away. I believe that this is a solid, unintended effect in the new pension system that we should be able to change. I don't understand – I can't understand – that it's good, social redistribution to reduce the pension to married people or those living together with low incomes and remove it for those with high incomes. It becomes completely incomprehensible to me. Finally, something that might seem even more pointless. See that Olga, who is a widow of 75 years, has Petra in 80 years, who is also widowed and deeply nurturing. If Olga finds out that she goes to Petra and cares for her, they have the same residence address. Then both of them will have a curtailment in the pension scheme. Where's the logic going? It is appropriate to better combine and eliminate these unintended effects. It cannot be intended to be that way, and on that basis I would like to take up the proposals of the Advance Party in the vote.

Nav is starting to find its shape – not infallibly, neither system nor employees, but it's doing better. The improvement of results is noticeable in several areas and user satisfaction is increasing. ICT modernisation is well under way, and the phase-in of new systems is now planned in the future. But everything is not in place. There's room for improvement, especially when it comes to getting people to work. There is a need for stronger and closer follow-up. Above all, there is a need for safe economic governance of Norway. The Labour Party chooses value creation rather than tax cuts, redistribution and social cohesion rather than giving more to those who have the most. The Labour Party sees the importance of good and predictable pay and employment conditions for employees, through strong tripartite cooperation, rather than using wages as a competitive tool. The government increases the activity obligation to receive transitional benefits rather than cut them. The government is betting on a workline for all, and not increasing the cash support level for anyone. This is largely related to the quality of Nav users' lives. Nav is about to become a learning organisation, and it's about to be. Important steps have been taken to improve the efficiency and quality of treatment and follow-up in Nav, and I want to say that the Committee has been involved in and a driver for maintaining the very high quality of the diversity of users that Nav has. Several proposals are on the table in this matter. The Government fraction of the Committee is clear that, at the very end of a growing period, this is not the time for major reorganisations. I refer to the majority's comments in the vote relating to the various proposals and the majority's position in the case.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4742	The proposal that is being promoted in this matter is about a very special group: the deaf blind. These are people with strong challenges. For most of us, it's hard to imagine what this disability means, where one of the senses reinforces the other. This means that without interpreters you have no opportunity to communicate with people around you, and there's almost no way to perceive what's happening where you are, if you don't get it told. At the same time, you have hardly enough opportunity to move out of the door without a companion. We have been able to read in the last year about deaf blind people who feel trapped in their own homes because of this intensification of the legislation. And some of us have been present at a very strong mark in Eidsvoll's place this morning, where 20-30 deaf blind people with their interpreters were present to make the Storting aware of their experiences with the system. It is difficult not to let the impressions I got today in the meetings with the representatives of the group, emotionally color this post. Imagine if someone had to tell me with sign language – whether it was drawn on my back or in my hand – everything that was said around me, everything that happened around me, that people smile at me, that people clapping for me, that someone nodding when I talk, or that something stands in the way of my legs. Imagine if all I can perceive on a blond with my relatively intact senses, had to be told. Then I would really feel what dependence on the services of society could mean. The background to this marker was a regulatory change introduced in the summer of 2012. Call it a ‘presation’, but it is perceived by deaf blind people as a severe restriction on their interpreting services. Whatever you call it, the result is the same: a large proportion of deaf blind people have been isolated, and their quality of life has been greatly reduced. The possibility of being part of society and participating in the same line with the others has been limited. It's a fact. In the Chamber today, we are therefore going to discuss a proposal from the Christian People's Party which aims to rectify this matter and which aims to reverse the change of rules that was made one year ago. The proposal promoted in the suspension shall ensure that deaf blind people are offered a full-fledged offer of interpreters/accompaniments for hobbies and leisure activities, regardless of whether it is to be done jointly with others or alone. It should ensure that the small group of deaf blind people – because there are no more than a few hundred in the whole country – will live a worthy life, a little more in freedom than they experience today. I have registered that it is granted a little over 8 million. additional to the interpreter service in the revised national budget. That's good, and it supports the Right. I hope the interpreters for the deaf blind get what they need from this pot, and it doesn't disappear into extra bureaucracy. At the same time, I record that the department and the directorate take self-criticism for the process that led up to the decision of last year. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4761	Today we have a proposal for a representative on the right of deaf blind people to interpret/accompaniment in hobbies and leisure activities. In examining the proposal, the Committee has given particular attention to the challenges faced by deaf blind people as a result of double sense loss. It gives this group very special communication challenges. Although activities are not to be carried out in conjunction with others, the need for communication can be crucial. Description of the environment, change of desired activity — you may change your mind — and dissemination of unexpected challenges are examples of communication needs that may arise, regardless of whether you have the activity with others or not. The deaf-blind group in Norway is small and presents special challenges. The background to the proposal for a representative is that a circular on interpreting and accompanying services was amended by the Summer 2012 Labour and Welfare Act. The change came without a prior discussion with the deaf-blind organisations and clarified the legal and legal basis applicable in this area, in particular the division of responsibilities between the State of the interpreters’ service and the local authority’s responsibilities for services. The Committee notes that the Council of State recognises the criticism of deaf-blind organisations for lack of process and involvement. The Committee also notes that the Ministry of Labour and Welfare has asked the Working and Welfare Department for a modest practice of the new guidelines. The organisations of the deaf blind, deaf organisations and interpreter organisations have been invited to a further process on the guidelines. Although the coverage of interpretative tasks in Norway is relatively good, nine out of ten tasks are being solved, there is a particular need for an increase in the number of interpreters and a better use of the resources available to us. It has been particularly difficult to obtain interpreters for short-term assignments, for example: medical sessions or parental meetings. The duration of the mission is not the same as that of the individual. That's why it's important that we find solutions for this. We assume that the process of the organisations concerned is being carried out with good involvement and with sufficient timescales for effective user involvement. That process shall lead to possible solutions. The increase in the area has been from 102 million. SEK in 2009 to SEK 140 million. kr in 2012. Nevertheless, we chose to add another 8.33 million. kr i revised for 2013 for this case. With regard to the change in the circular, it was also clarified that in the event of rejection of the assignment to which interpreters had previously been granted, there would be a dialogue with the municipality to find appropriate solutions. The Employment and Welfare Act was also intended to assist municipalities in training, enabling them to assist deaf-blind people in line with their actual responsibilities. The criteria must be met. And assuming that, we're being taken care of by the department. We are also aware that there are counties that have not implemented the circular, precisely because the interaction and training has not been in place. The basis of public interest for the interpretation service is based on Sections 10 to 6, which deal with daily life. It does not initially include training, sports and hobbies, but it is an exception that still warrants this if there are activities that are so large that they are considered part of daily life. Then there is a social benefit anyway. We expect that this is what has provided the basis for the practice that has been used until the summer of 2012. This may indicate that the practice did not have a sufficient basis in the law and that a change in the circular has to occur as a result of a change in the current legal basis. We believe that it is not right for the Storting now to ask the Working and Welfare Department to change the circular back to a practice that we have found out is not allowed. Then we may have to go through a change in the law that makes the law in line with the practice of Storting. The justification for the red-green fraction’s proposal that this should be annexed to the Protocol today is that this needs to be clarified. That work is already in progress, and we expect that if there is a need for legislative changes, it will be brought back to the Storting in an appropriate form. Therefore, it has been decided to clarify that even if it is outside the law, this should be treated as if it were under the old guidelines. It's going far beyond what the Storting tends to ask a government to do. This matter has been so special that we have chosen to do it anyway. I would like to raise the suggestion that we have raised in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e4855	The system of interpreting and accompanying assistance for deaf blind people has been highly debated in recent years, among users and in the political environment. The debate was triggered by a clarification in the guidelines of the Working and Welfare Act in the summer of 2012. The representative proposal we are discussing today is to confirm that deaf blind people will be given a right to interpreting and accompanying assistance, for all activities, i.e. hobbies and leisure activities, regardless of whether the activity is to be done jointly with others. As the proposer points out, deaf blind people are a special group with special needs. The need to communicate with the outside world is different from that of other groups. They may be going to a doctor or a meeting. Especially for a part that lives far away from central strokes, the offer today is so that those who are freelancers are not fully paid for travelling time to deaf blind people who live very far away. In order to strengthen the situation of the deaf blind, it is therefore important to have more permanent staff interpreters in Nav who can be included in a watch list, so that it becomes predictable. It is also important to look at the pay and work conditions of freelance bars, so that they can help deaf blind people across the country. SEK in 2009 to SEK 140 million. kr in 2012 and there has been an increase in the revised national budget of 8.3 million. kr. It has given a better offer, but it is far from good enough. For some users — not all — this meant that they experienced changes and that the offer was worse than they had had. That is why we have said that no one should be deprived of the offer they have had in the past, because we are now going to review the guidelines again. That is why we are in a process around this. Both Nav and the Ministry of Labour have taken self-criticism for the way this has been done, but what was meant was to ensure uniform treatment across Norway, so that no deaf-blind person in one part of the country had a completely different offer than others, and to ensure that many more were helped with what they experienced as their everyday problems. On the one hand, extensive dialogue has been established with users and their municipalities in all cases where social security benefits under the Law on Social Security cannot be provided. I have started a work to review the guidelines together with the deaf-blind organisations. I want to hear their views on how best to use interpretative resources. It has been claimed that the changes have been very influential in the possibility for deaf blind people to participate. That's not the intention of the changes. The deaf blind must continue to be able to be active, including those who spend a lot of time on leisure activities. I believe that the work that has now started will help to make this possible.

It's almost 100 years since the 70s were introduced, and working life and life is something quite different now than it was then. It is high time to adapt the law not only to the pension reform but also to the fact that we not only live longer but are also healthy longer. Therefore, I am somewhat disappointed that we have not been able to reach a similar compromise on special age limits. I share the impatience of the proposers in this matter, and therefore the Right, together with the Progress Party, promotes a proposal that the special age limits should also be explored. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e5350	The representative Robert Eriksson has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e5353	The representatives of Storting Robert Eriksson, Anders Anundsen and Harald T. Nesvik have proposed that the resignation of employees who have crossed the special age limit should cease in state companies and establishments where the State is the sole owner or the majority shareholder. Furthermore, the government is asked to provide a specific review of the specific age limits in the public and private sectors, as required by the pension reform. In the Act on Age Limits for Officials, the general age limit is 70 years. Reached age includes both an obligation to resign from the job and a right to a pension. However, the age limit may be set below 70 years if the position imposes exceptional physical or mental stress, so that the individual normally does not have the power to perform the work properly until the age of 70. Alternatively, the position is required to set specific requirements for physical and mental characteristics which are normally more severely impaired before the age of 70 than the proper performance of the service. The lower age limit than 70 is often referred to as the special age limit. The main reason for this special age limit is therefore to protect the employer and society from the consequence that someone is in a position unresponsive for a long time. Most specific age limits in the public sector were set a good many years ago. In many cases, the content of the posts concerned is different from the time the age limit was set. Manual tasks may have been automated. Furthermore, life expectancy in the population has increased over time, and there is much evidence that the health status of older age groups has improved regularly. In many cases, it may at least be possible and appropriate to go beyond what has been foreseen at the time of the establishment of the current special age limits. A central issue of pension reform is to get people to work longer. Continuing a system with a lower specific age limit for a large occupational group does not support this objective. I therefore agree with the rapporteur that there should be a review of the specific age limit in the public and private sectors, and aim at starting such work. The review should also cover the so-called 85-year rule, which means that an old-age pension can be drawn up up up to three years before the age limit, if the sum of age and length of service is at least 85 years. What I'm skeptical about is removing the obligation to withdraw for those with special age limits without seeing this in conjunction with other parts of the legislation, such as. the right to a pension. I also believe that it is not appropriate to limit any adjustments in the special age limit to state companies and businesses in which the State is the sole owner and joint owner. Account must also be taken of the fact that the special age limits involve both rights and obligations for those covered. Therefore, changes in the legislation of persons with special age limits should be addressed in consultation with social partners, not by a single decision of the Stortinget. Such a review must also address the need for any transitional arrangements.
