It must be an improper paradox for a government that promised to eliminate poverty, that after eight years of government, it has become more poor children in Norway. According to the SSB, 74 000 children under 18 now live in families with persistently low incomes. There are 6,500 more than when this government took over power in 2005. I will not extend this debate. I do not claim that the Left has an answer to all the problems, but we have today outlined 14 different actions in connection with the Innst. 481 S which can help reduce poverty. With this, I address the Left's proposals that have been distributed in the Chamber. In addition, we are voting in favour of the proposal No. One, two, three, and thirteen in the same case.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e5560	It's noted. The representative Borghild Tenden has taken up the suggestions she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-18.ana.ud792e5563	I would like to thank the proposers for their proposals. This government has put poverty, and especially poverty related to children, high on the political agenda. I understand that the proposers support this agenda and the importance of an ongoing discussion on which action should and can be taken. The two representative proposals contain a number of individual proposals. Each individual proposal was commented on in my written replies to the Committee. Many of the income areas proposed to be strengthened are strengthened — or activity is under way — under this government. So to the extent that the representative of the Red Isaac says that there are a lot of tips to pick up for the government, I think that many of the proposals promoted by the opposition are marked by being tips they have received from the government itself. One example of this was when I was Minister of Culture and we supported cultural maps, which are about the municipality going ahead and giving free access to cinema, swimming pool, museums and theatres. It has not cost the municipality anything, because they have used existing offers, but it is one example that poverty in our time is less about having no money for food. It's about the lack of common experiences, the fact that there are some kids and young people who don't have anything to tell from the weekend, who don't participate in the swimming pool and leisure activities, like other kids. We have strengthened many of these arrangements, including: activity and participation for vulnerable children, under the direction of non-governmental organisations. We have also received criticism from part of the opposition parties that we are too instrumental in our view of the voluntary organisations. They believe that they should have a role in terms of inclusion. But I believe that this is precisely the role of voluntary organisations. A pinch of individual measures — if they are never that good — cannot solve the poverty challenges alone. The proposers behind the proposals for action against child poverty sum up that the most important measures in the long term will be a socially equalising school and work life with room for all. I fully agree with that, but with their proposals, the proposers will give priority to targeted action against those who need it most, rather than broad schemes. I do not entirely agree with that, as I believe that poverty must be combated in both the short and the long term. I believe that the Nordic welfare model with strong Community solutions and a universal safety network have contributed to a balanced distribution of income and to a limited level of poverty. An example of free cultural lessons for all is important because it is universal. It not only helps those who have the least, but it is an offer for everyone. Preventing poverty prevents the transfer of poverty to new generations. We therefore need to look at what conditions in society, what social structures, which help to create and maintain social and economic differences, and then pay and strong links with employment are the most important single factor preventing poverty. At the EU's low income level, a family could be defined as poor if you have three children, mother is at home, and father earns 600 000 or less – then one less than the low income limit. The work of the government is therefore aimed at giving more opportunities to choose work and activity rather than benefits, and the qualification programme is an example of a targeted measure that allows long-term beneficiaries of benefits to get into work. Another is Jobjesen, which Andersen, a representative, has talked about warmly today, which is an example of measures that allow immigrant women at home to participate in training and qualification. I believe welfare schemes need to be designed to support the goal of working for all. A real, reasonable and good range of childcare facilities and the SFO are examples of societal priorities that are necessary to ensure that the choice is no longer between work or care. Social benefits must also be designed in such a way that it is more profitable to work than to receive social security. Therefore, it is incomprehensible that the opposition wants to increase and extend the cash support, all the time we know that among those at risk of living in low-income families, mother is often out of work. However, the government’s long-term efforts to combat poverty need to be combined with short-term measures to help children who fall outside, here and now — children who are not allowed to go on a summer holiday or to participate in birthdays. The government will therefore target and strengthen schemes that meet the needs of the various children to participate in leisure activities and holiday services.
